<a id='question-017'></a>

### Question 017: Metabolic Disease: Pompe Disease (GSD II)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Lysosomal storage disease caused by deficiency of acid alpha-1,4-glucosidase (acid maltase), leading to glycogen accumulation in lysosomes, particularly affecting cardiac and skeletal muscle, resulting in hypertrophic cardiomyopathy and muscle weakness. (由酸性α-1,4-葡萄糖苷酶（酸性麦芽酶）缺乏引起的溶酶体贮积病，导致溶酶体中糖原积累，主要影响心脏和骨骼肌，导致肥厚型心肌病和肌肉无力。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 6-month-old male infant presents to the pediatrician with progressive feeding difficulties, failure to thrive, and generalized muscle weakness. His mother reports that he has difficulty lifting his head and rolling over, milestones he had previously achieved. Vital signs show a respiratory rate of 35 breaths/min and heart rate of 180 bpm. Physical examination reveals significant hypotonia, macroglossia, and a markedly enlarged abdomen. Auscultation reveals a loud S4 gallop. An echocardiogram shows severe, global hypertrophic cardiomyopathy with marked left ventricular wall thickening (2.5 cm) and impaired systolic function. A skeletal muscle biopsy reveals enlarged, pale-staining muscle fibers with abundant vacuolar inclusions. Electron microscopy confirms the presence of lysosomal glycogen accumulation. Enzyme assay of leukocytes shows markedly reduced acid alpha-1,4-glucosidase activity (less than 1% of normal).

Which of the following is the underlying defect responsible for this patient's condition?

(A) Deficiency of branched-chain alpha-1,4-glucosidase leading to accumulation of branched glycogen in lysosomes.
(B) Deficiency of acid alpha-1,4-glucosidase leading to accumulation of glycogen in lysosomes.
(C) Deficiency of beta-1,4-galactosyltransferase leading to accumulation of GM2 ganglioside in lysosomes.
(D) Deficiency of hexosaminidase A leading to accumulation of GM2 ganglioside in lysosomes.
(E) Deficiency of glucocerebrosidase leading to accumulation of glucocerebroside in lysosomes.

#### Correct Answer: B

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 6-month-old male infant. This age is typical for the infantile-onset form of Pompe disease.
- **Presenting Symptoms:** Progressive feeding difficulties, failure to thrive, generalized muscle weakness (hypotonia), difficulty lifting head/rolling over (developmental regression). These point towards a severe neuromuscular disorder.
- **Physical Exam:** Hypotonia, macroglossia (enlarged tongue), enlarged abdomen (hepatomegaly often present), loud S4 gallop (indicative of stiff, hypertrophied ventricle). These findings strongly suggest significant cardiac and skeletal muscle involvement.
- **Echocardiogram:** Severe, global hypertrophic cardiomyopathy with LV wall thickening (2.5 cm) and impaired systolic function. This is a hallmark feature of infantile Pompe disease.
- **Muscle Biopsy:** Enlarged, pale-staining muscle fibers with vacuolar inclusions. Electron microscopy confirms lysosomal glycogen accumulation. This points to a lysosomal storage disorder involving glycogen.
- **Enzyme Assay:** Markedly reduced acid alpha-1,4-glucosidase activity. This is the definitive diagnostic test for Pompe disease.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the underlying defect. Based on the clinical presentation, biopsy findings, and enzyme assay, the defect is a deficiency of acid alpha-1,4-glucosidase leading to lysosomal glycogen accumulation.

##### 2. Pathophysiological, Molecular & Pharmacological Deep-Dive (病理生理、分子机制与药理靶点深度剖析)
- **Pompe Disease (Glycogen Storage Disease Type II):** An autosomal recessive lysosomal storage disorder.
- **Defective Enzyme:** Acid alpha-1,4-glucosidase (also known as acid maltase or GAA). This enzyme is located in the lysosomes and is responsible for breaking down glycogen into glucose.
- **Molecular Basis:** Mutations in the *GAA* gene on chromosome 15q25.2 lead to deficient or non-functional enzyme.
- **Pathophysiology:** Lack of functional acid alpha-1,4-glucosidase prevents the breakdown of glycogen within lysosomes. Glycogen accumulates progressively in lysosomes of various tissues, particularly cardiac muscle, skeletal muscle, and liver.
- **Clinical Manifestations:** The accumulation of glycogen disrupts normal cellular function, leading to cellular damage and organ dysfunction. In the infantile form, massive glycogen accumulation in cardiomyocytes causes severe hypertrophic cardiomyopathy, leading to heart failure and death, typically within the first year of life. Skeletal muscle involvement causes profound hypotonia and weakness.
- **Diagnosis:** Enzyme assay (measuring GAA activity in leukocytes or fibroblasts), genetic testing (*GAA* gene mutations), muscle biopsy (showing vacuolar inclusions and glycogen accumulation).

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): Deficiency of branched-chain alpha-1,4-glucosidase leading to accumulation of branched glycogen in lysosomes. This describes **Andersen disease (GSD IV)**. Andersen disease involves a deficiency of the 4-alpha-glucanotransferase enzyme, leading to the accumulation of abnormal, branched glycogen (limit dextrin) in lysosomes. While it causes liver cirrhosis and hypotonia, it typically does not present with the severe hypertrophic cardiomyopathy seen in Pompe disease. The enzyme defect is different.
- (C): Deficiency of acid beta-1,4-galactosyltransferase leading to accumulation of GM2 ganglioside in lysosomes. This describes **GM2 gangliosidosis (Sandhoff disease)**, an autosomal recessive lysosomal storage disorder. It presents with progressive neurodegeneration, cherry-red spot on the macula, and hepatosplenomegaly. It does not typically cause severe hypertrophic cardiomyopathy or skeletal muscle weakness as the primary features. The accumulated substance is GM2 ganglioside, not glycogen.
- (D): Deficiency of hexosaminidase A leading to accumulation of GM2 ganglioside in lysosomes. This describes **Tay-Sachs disease**, another autosomal recessive lysosomal storage disorder. It primarily affects the central nervous system, causing progressive neurodegeneration, developmental regression, seizures, and a cherry-red spot on the macula. It does not cause hypertrophic cardiomyopathy or significant skeletal muscle weakness. The accumulated substance is GM2 ganglioside, not glycogen.
- (E): Deficiency of glucocerebrosidase leading to accumulation of glucocerebroside in lysosomes. This describes **Gaucher disease**, the most common lysosomal storage disorder. It is caused by mutations in the *GBA* gene. Type 1 (non-neuronopathic) typically presents with hepatosplenomegaly, bone disease, and cytopenias. Types 2 and 3 (neuronopathic) involve neurological symptoms. While cardiac involvement can occur, severe hypertrophic cardiomyopathy is not the characteristic presentation, especially in infancy. The accumulated substance is glucocerebroside, not glycogen.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点横向对比与鉴别矩阵):
| Feature                 | Pompe Disease (GSD II) | Andersen Disease (GSD IV) | Tay-Sachs Disease | Gaucher Disease | GM2 Gangliosidosis (Sandhoff) |
|-------------------------|------------------------|---------------------------|-------------------|-----------------|--------------------------------|
| **Enzyme Deficiency**   | Acid α-1,4-Glucosidase | 4-α-Glucanotransferase   | Hexosaminidase A  | Glucocerebrosidase | Acid β-1,4-Galactosyltransferase |
| **Accumulated Substance**| Lysosomal Glycogen     | Branched Glycogen (Limit Dextrin) | GM2 Ganglioside   | Glucocerebroside | GM2 Ganglioside                 |
| **Primary Organs Affected**| Heart, Skeletal Muscle | Liver, Muscle             | CNS               | Spleen, Liver, Bone | CNS                             |
| **Key Clinical Feature**| Hypertrophic Cardiomyopathy, Hypotonia | Liver Cirrhosis, Hypotonia | Neurodegeneration, Cherry-Red Spot | Hepatosplenomegaly, Bone Pain | Neurodegeneration, Cherry-Red Spot |
| **Age of Onset (Infantile)**| < 1 year               | Variable                  | 3-6 months        | Variable        | 3-6 months                      |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Pompe disease (GSD II) is a lysosomal storage disorder caused by acid alpha-1,4-glucosidase deficiency, leading to glycogen accumulation, particularly in cardiac and skeletal muscle. The infantile form presents with severe hypertrophic cardiomyopathy, hypotonia, and early death. Key differentials include other glycogen storage diseases (like Andersen disease) and other lysosomal storage diseases (like Tay-Sachs, Gaucher, Sandhoff) which affect different enzymes, accumulate different substances, and have distinct clinical presentations. Always consider the specific enzyme defect, accumulated substance, and primary organ involvement when differentiating these conditions.

<a id='question-018'></a>

### Question 018: Metabolic Disease: Cori Disease (GSD III)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Glycogen Storage Disease Type III (Cori Disease) is caused by a deficiency in the debranching enzyme (amylo-1,6-glucosidase), leading to the accumulation of abnormal glycogen (limit dextrin) in the liver and muscle, resulting in hypoglycemia, hepatomegaly, and sometimes muscle weakness. Unlike GSD I (von Gierke), lactate levels are typically normal or only mildly elevated because gluconeogenesis is intact. (糖原累积症III型（Cori病）是由脱支酶（淀粉样糖-1,6-葡萄糖苷酶）缺乏引起的，导致肝脏和肌肉中异常糖原（限支淀粉）积累，从而导致低血糖、肝脏肿大，有时还会出现肌肉无力。与糖原累积症I型（von Gierke病）不同，乳酸水平通常正常或仅轻度升高，因为糖异生是完整的。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 3-year-old boy presents to the emergency department with recurrent episodes of hypoglycemia, particularly during overnight fasting. His parents report that he becomes lethargic, irritable, and sometimes has seizures when his blood sugar drops. Physical examination reveals hepatomegaly and mild hypotonia. Laboratory investigations show fasting hypoglycemia (glucose 45 mg/dL, normal range 70-100 mg/dL), elevated liver enzymes (AST 180 U/L, ALT 150 U/L, normal ranges <40 U/L), normal lactate levels (1.5 mmol/L, normal range 0.5-2.0 mmol/L), and normal renal function. A liver biopsy shows accumulation of abnormal glycogen with short outer branches, visualized as a pale, granular material on PAS staining. The glycogen is resistant to digestion by both amylo-1,4-glucosidase and amylo-1,6-glucosidase in vitro. Which of the following is the most likely underlying defect?

(A) Deficiency of glucose-6-phosphatase, leading to impaired gluconeogenesis and glycogenolysis.
(B) Deficiency of amylo-1,4-glucosidase, resulting in accumulation of intact glycogen granules.
(C) Deficiency of the debranching enzyme (amylo-1,6-glucosidase), impairing the breakdown of glycogen outer branches.
(D) Deficiency of phosphofructokinase-1, causing impaired glycolysis and glycogen synthesis.
(E) Deficiency of pyruvate dehydrogenase complex, leading to lactic acidosis and impaired pyruvate metabolism.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
*   **Patient Profile:** 3-year-old boy with recurrent hypoglycemia, especially during fasting. This points towards a glycogen storage disease or other metabolic disorder affecting glucose homeostasis.
*   **Symptoms:** Lethargy, irritability, seizures during hypoglycemia. Classic signs of severe hypoglycemia.
*   **Physical Exam:** Hepatomegaly (enlarged liver) and mild hypotonia (decreased muscle tone). Hepatomegaly suggests glycogen accumulation in the liver. Hypotonia can occur in some GSDs affecting muscle.
*   **Labs:** Fasting hypoglycemia (glucose 45 mg/dL). Confirms the primary problem is low blood sugar. Elevated liver enzymes (AST/ALT) suggest liver damage or stress, often seen with glycogen storage. Normal lactate levels (1.5 mmol/L). This is a crucial clue differentiating GSD III from GSD I (von Gierke disease), where lactate is typically elevated due to impaired gluconeogenesis.
*   **Liver Biopsy:** Accumulation of abnormal glycogen with short outer branches. PAS staining shows pale, granular material. This abnormal glycogen structure is characteristic of limit dextrin. The glycogen is resistant to digestion by amylo-1,4-glucosidase (which breaks down the linear chains) but also resistant to amylo-1,6-glucosidase (the debranching enzyme). This specific pattern strongly suggests a defect in the debranching enzyme.
*   **Question Stem:** Asks for the underlying defect based on the clinical and pathological findings.

##### 2. Pathophysiological, Molecular & Pharmacological Deep-Dive (病理生理、分子机制与药理靶点深度剖析)
*   **Glycogen Metabolism:** Glycogen is a branched polymer of glucose. Glycogenolysis (breakdown) involves two key enzymes: amylo-1,4-glucosidase (breaks α-1,4 linkages) and the debranching enzyme (amylo-1,6-glucosidase and 4-α-glucanotransferase; breaks α-1,6 linkages at branch points).
*   **GSD III (Cori Disease):** Caused by deficiency of the debranching enzyme (encoded by the *AGL* gene). This enzyme has two activities: transferase and glucosidase. The deficiency prevents the complete breakdown of glycogen.
*   **Pathology:** Glycogen accumulates in the liver and muscle. The accumulated glycogen is structurally abnormal, consisting mainly of short outer chains attached to branch points (limit dextrin). This abnormal glycogen is poorly soluble and causes cellular swelling and organomegaly.
*   **Clinical Manifestations:** Fasting hypoglycemia (due to impaired glycogenolysis), hepatomegaly (due to glycogen accumulation in the liver), muscle weakness/hypotonia (due to glycogen accumulation in muscle, though often less severe than in other GSDs), and sometimes cardiomyopathy.
*   **Lab Findings:** Hypoglycemia, elevated liver enzymes, normal or mildly elevated lactate (unlike GSD I where lactate is markedly elevated due to impaired gluconeogenesis).
*   **Biopsy:** Shows limit dextrin accumulation, resistant to both amylo-1,4-glucosidase and amylo-1,6-glucosidase digestion.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
*   **(A) Deficiency of glucose-6-phosphatase:** This causes GSD Type I (von Gierke disease). Key features include severe fasting hypoglycemia, hepatomegaly, lactic acidosis (due to impaired gluconeogenesis), hyperuricemia, and hyperlipidemia. The normal lactate in this vignette rules out GSD I.
*   **(B) Deficiency of amylo-1,4-glucosidase:** This causes GSD Type IV (Andersen disease). Key features include hepatomegaly, progressive liver cirrhosis, and often failure to thrive. The glycogen accumulates as normal-appearing granules but is structurally abnormal (long outer chains). The biopsy finding of short outer branches (limit dextrin) is not typical for GSD IV.
*   **(D) Deficiency of phosphofructokinase-1 (PFK-1):** This causes a glycolytic defect (Tarui disease, GSD VII). It primarily affects muscle, causing exercise intolerance, muscle cramps, and myoglobinuria. Liver involvement and hypoglycemia are less common or absent. The biopsy findings are inconsistent with GSD VII.
*   **(E) Deficiency of pyruvate dehydrogenase complex:** This causes a defect in pyruvate metabolism, leading to lactic acidosis (as pyruvate cannot be converted to acetyl-CoA), neurological dysfunction, and often hypoglycemia. The normal lactate level in the vignette makes this diagnosis unlikely.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点横向对比与鉴别矩阵):
| Feature                 | GSD I (von Gierke) | GSD III (Cori) | GSD IV (Andersen) | GSD VII (Tarui) |
| :---------------------- | :---------------- | :------------- | :--------------- | :------------- |
| Enzyme Defect           | Glucose-6-Pase    | Debranching    | Amylo-1,4-Gluc   | PFK-1          |
| Gene                    | *G6PC*            | *AGL*          | *PYGL*           | *PFKFB3*       |
| Glycogen Structure      | Normal            | Limit Dextrin  | Normal Granules  | Normal         |
| Liver Involvement       | Yes (Hepatomegaly) | Yes            | Yes (Cirrhosis)  | Minimal        |
| Muscle Involvement      | No                | Yes            | No               | Yes            |
| Hypoglycemia            | Severe            | Moderate       | Moderate         | Mild/Absent    |
| Lactic Acidosis         | Yes               | No/Mild        | No               | No             |
| Key Biopsy Finding      | Normal Glycogen   | Limit Dextrin  | Normal Granules  | Normal         |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Cori disease (GSD III) is characterized by a deficiency in the debranching enzyme, leading to the accumulation of limit dextrin in the liver and muscle, causing fasting hypoglycemia and hepatomegaly. Normal lactate levels help differentiate it from GSD I (von Gierke), which features lactic acidosis due to impaired gluconeogenesis. The biopsy finding of limit dextrin is pathognomonic. (Cori病（糖原累积症III型）的特点是脱支酶缺乏，导致肝脏和肌肉中限支淀粉积累，引起空腹性低血糖和肝脏肿大。正常的乳酸水平有助于将其与糖原累积症I型（von Gierke病）区分开来，后者由于糖异生受损而出现乳酸增多症。限支淀粉的活检发现是其特征。)

<a id='question-019'></a>

### Question 019: Metabolic Disease: Von Gierke Disease (GSD I)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Von Gierke disease (Glycogen Storage Disease Type I) is caused by a deficiency in glucose-6-phosphatase, leading to impaired glycogenolysis and gluconeogenesis, resulting in severe fasting hypoglycemia, lactic acidosis, hyperuricemia, hyperlipidemia, and hepatomegaly. (冯·吉尔克病（I型糖原病）是由葡萄糖-6-磷酸酶缺乏引起的，导致糖异生和糖原降解受损，从而引起严重的空腹低血糖、乳酸酸中毒、高尿酸血症、高脂血症和肝脏肿大。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 6-month-old male infant presents to the emergency department with lethargy, poor feeding, and vomiting for the past 12 hours. His mother reports that these symptoms typically occur after he has not eaten for several hours. Vital signs show a heart rate of 140 bpm, respiratory rate of 30 breaths/min, blood pressure of 80/50 mmHg, and temperature of 37.0°C. Physical examination reveals a pale, thin infant with a protuberant abdomen, hepatomegaly (liver edge palpable 8 cm below the right costal margin), and a characteristic "doll-like" facial appearance (frontal bossing, thin extremities, cherubic face). Laboratory studies show a blood glucose level of 35 mg/dL (normal range: 70-100 mg/dL), elevated lactate level of 6.0 mmol/L (normal range: 0.5-2.0 mmol/L), elevated uric acid level of 8.0 mg/dL (normal range: 2.5-6.0 mg/dL), and hypertriglyceridemia (triglycerides 450 mg/dL, normal range: <150 mg/dL). Liver biopsy shows marked glycogen accumulation within hepatocytes, with no evidence of abnormal storage material other than glycogen. Which of the following is the underlying defect in this patient's metabolic pathway?

(A) Deficiency of phosphorylase, leading to impaired glycogen breakdown in muscle.
(B) Deficiency of the glucose-6-phosphatase enzyme system, impairing the final step of gluconeogenesis and glycogenolysis.
(C) Deficiency of branched-chain α-1,4-glucanotransferase (debranching enzyme), resulting in the accumulation of abnormally structured glycogen.
(D) Deficiency of lysosomal α-1,4-glucosidase (acid maltase), leading to glycogen accumulation within lysosomes.
(E) Deficiency of galactokinase, causing accumulation of galactitol in tissues.

#### Correct Answer: B

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
*   **Patient Profile:** 6-month-old male infant. Age is typical for presentation of inborn errors of metabolism.
*   **Presenting Symptoms:** Lethargy, poor feeding, vomiting after fasting (12 hours). Classic signs of hypoglycemia.
*   **Vital Signs:** Tachycardia (140 bpm), hypotension (80/50 mmHg). Suggestive of metabolic stress and potential shock secondary to hypoglycemia.
*   **Physical Exam:** Pale, thin, protuberant abdomen, hepatomegaly (8 cm below RCM), "doll-like" face (frontal bossing, cherubic features). Hepatomegaly is due to glycogen accumulation. Doll-like face is characteristic of Von Gierke disease. Thinness suggests chronic metabolic derangement.
*   **Laboratory Findings:**
    *   **Hypoglycemia (35 mg/dL):** Key finding, exacerbated by fasting. Indicates impaired glucose production/release.
    *   **Lactic Acidemia (6.0 mmol/L):** Elevated lactate suggests impaired gluconeogenesis (lactate is a gluconeogenic substrate) and/or impaired lactate clearance by the liver.
    *   **Hyperuricemia (8.0 mg/dL):** Increased uric acid production due to increased purine nucleotide degradation (ATP breakdown) as the body tries to compensate for low glucose. Also, impaired renal excretion of uric acid.
    *   **Hypertriglyceridemia (450 mg/dL):** Increased VLDL synthesis in the liver due to excess substrate (lactate, pyruvate) and impaired fatty acid oxidation.
*   **Liver Biopsy:** Marked glycogen accumulation in hepatocytes. Confirms a glycogen storage disease affecting the liver. No abnormal storage material other than glycogen rules out other GSDs with abnormal structures.
*   **Question Stem:** Asks for the underlying defect in the metabolic pathway.

These findings (severe fasting hypoglycemia, lactic acidosis, hepatomegaly, doll-like face, hyperuricemia, hyperlipidemia, glycogen accumulation in liver) are pathognomonic for Von Gierke disease (Glycogen Storage Disease Type I).

##### 2. Pathophysiological, Molecular & Pharmacological Deep-Dive (病理生理、分子机制与药理靶点深度剖析)
*   **Von Gierke Disease (GSD I):** Caused by a deficiency in the glucose-6-phosphatase enzyme system. This system is located primarily in the liver and kidney cortex and is responsible for hydrolyzing glucose-6-phosphate (G6P) into free glucose and inorganic phosphate (Pi).
*   **Mechanism:**
    1.  **Impaired Glycogenolysis:** G6P is the immediate product of glycogen breakdown (by glycogen phosphorylase). Without glucose-6-phosphatase, G6P cannot be converted to free glucose, preventing the release of glucose from the liver into the bloodstream during fasting. This leads to severe fasting hypoglycemia.
    2.  **Impaired Gluconeogenesis:** G6P is also a key intermediate in gluconeogenesis (the synthesis of glucose from non-carbohydrate precursors like lactate, pyruvate, alanine, glycerol). The final step of gluconeogenesis, the conversion of G6P to free glucose, is blocked. This contributes to hypoglycemia and the accumulation of gluconeogenic precursors.
    3.  **Lactic Acidosis:** The accumulation of gluconeogenic precursors, particularly lactate and pyruvate, leads to lactic acidosis. The liver cannot convert these substrates into glucose.
    4.  **Hyperuricemia:** Increased ATP degradation (due to energy deficit) leads to increased purine breakdown and uric acid production. Impaired renal excretion of uric acid also contributes.
    5.  **Hyperlipidemia:** Increased synthesis of VLDL (very-low-density lipoprotein) in the liver due to excess acetyl-CoA (derived from pyruvate) and fatty acids.
    6.  **Hepatomegaly:** Massive accumulation of glycogen in the liver due to the inability to break down glycogen or release glucose.
*   **Molecular Basis:** GSD Ia is caused by mutations in the *G6PC* gene, which encodes the catalytic subunit (G6Pase α) of the glucose-6-phosphatase enzyme complex. GSD Ib is caused by mutations in the *SLC37A4* gene, which encodes the glucose-6-phosphate transporter (G6PT), responsible for transporting G6P into the endoplasmic reticulum where the enzyme complex resides. Both result in a functional deficiency of the enzyme system.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
*   **(A) Deficiency of phosphorylase, leading to impaired glycogen breakdown in muscle.** This describes McArdle disease (GSD V). McArdle disease affects muscle phosphorylase (myophosphorylase), causing exercise intolerance, muscle cramps, and myoglobinuria. It does *not* typically cause severe fasting hypoglycemia, lactic acidosis, hepatomegaly, or the doll-like face seen in GSD I. Liver glycogenolysis is primarily affected by G6Pase, not muscle phosphorylase.
*   **(C) Deficiency of branched-chain α-1,4-glucanotransferase (debranching enzyme), resulting in the accumulation of abnormally structured glycogen.** This describes Andersen disease (GSD IV). GSD IV affects the debranching enzyme, leading to the accumulation of glycogen with short outer branches (limit dextrin). It causes progressive liver cirrhosis, hepatosplenomegaly, and failure to thrive. While it involves glycogen accumulation, the specific enzyme defect and clinical presentation (cirrhosis vs. doll-like face, severe hypoglycemia) are different from GSD I.
*   **(D) Deficiency of lysosomal α-1,4-glucosidase (acid maltase), leading to glycogen accumulation within lysosomes.** This describes Pompe disease (GSD II). Pompe disease affects the lysosomal enzyme acid α-glucosidase (GAA), leading to glycogen accumulation within lysosomes in various tissues, particularly muscle (cardiomyopathy, skeletal muscle weakness). The infantile form presents with severe cardiomyopathy and hypotonia. It does not typically cause fasting hypoglycemia, lactic acidosis, or hepatomegaly as the primary features. Glycogen accumulates in lysosomes, not the cytoplasm.
*   **(E) Deficiency of galactokinase, causing accumulation of galactitol in tissues.** This describes Galactosemia (specifically, classic galactosemia due to galactose-1-phosphate uridyltransferase deficiency, or galactokinase deficiency). Galactosemia involves impaired metabolism of galactose. Galactokinase deficiency leads to accumulation of galactose and galactitol (formed by aldose reductase) in tissues like the lens (causing cataracts) and brain. It presents with feeding difficulties, vomiting, jaundice, hepatomegaly, and cataracts, but not the specific pattern of severe fasting hypoglycemia, lactic acidosis, hyperuricemia, and hyperlipidemia seen in GSD I.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点横向对比与鉴别矩阵):
| Feature                 | Von Gierke (GSD I)                                  | McArdle (GSD V)                                     | Andersen (GSD IV)                                  | Pompe (GSD II)                                    | Galactosemia (Galactokinase Def.)                |
| :---------------------- | :-------------------------------------------------- | :-------------------------------------------------- | :-------------------------------------------------- | :-------------------------------------------------- | :-------------------------------------------------- |
| **Primary Defect**      | Glucose-6-phosphatase                               | Muscle phosphorylase                                | Debranching enzyme (α-1,4-glucanotransferase)       | Lysosomal α-1,4-glucosidase (Acid Maltase)         | Galactokinase                                       |
| **Location of Defect**  | Liver, Kidney                                       | Muscle                                              | Liver                                               | Lysosomes (various tissues, esp. muscle)            | Liver, Lens, Brain                                   |
| **Key Metabolic Issue** | Impaired glycogenolysis & gluconeogenesis           | Impaired muscle glycogenolysis                       | Abnormal glycogen structure, impaired glycogenolysis | Lysosomal glycogen accumulation                    | Galactose metabolism defect                         |
| **Hypoglycemia**        | Severe fasting hypoglycemia                          | Normal fasting glucose                               | Normal fasting glucose                               | Normal fasting glucose                               | Normal fasting glucose                               |
| **Lactic Acidosis**     | Yes                                                 | No                                                  | No                                                  | No                                                  | No                                                  |
| **Hepatomegaly**        | Yes (massive glycogen accumulation)                  | No                                                  | Yes (cirrhosis)                                     | No                                                  | Yes                                                 |
| **Muscle Involvement**  | No (except secondary effects)                        | Yes (exercise intolerance, cramps)                   | No                                                  | Yes (cardiomyopathy, weakness)                     | No                                                  |
| **Characteristic Face** | Doll-like (frontal bossing, cherubic)               | No                                                  | No                                                  | No                                                  | No                                                  |
| **Other Labs**          | Hyperuricemia, Hyperlipidemia (high TG)             | Elevated CK, Myoglobinuria after exercise            | Liver failure markers                                | Elevated CK                                         | Cataracts (Galactokinase Def.), Jaundice, E. coli sepsis |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Von Gierke disease (GSD I) is characterized by a deficiency in glucose-6-phosphatase, leading to impaired release of glucose from the liver and kidneys. This results in severe fasting hypoglycemia, lactic acidosis, hyperuricemia, hyperlipidemia, and hepatomegaly. Remember to differentiate it from other glycogen storage diseases based on the specific enzyme defect, tissue involvement, and clinical presentation (e.g., McArdle disease affects muscle, Pompe disease affects lysosomes, Andersen disease causes cirrhosis).

<a id='question-020'></a>

### Question 20: Metabolic Disease: McArdle Disease (GSD V)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: McArdle disease (Glycogen Storage Disease Type V) is caused by a deficiency in skeletal muscle glycogen phosphorylase, leading to impaired glycogenolysis in muscle, exercise intolerance, muscle cramps, myoglobinuria, and the "second-wind phenomenon" due to increased reliance on fatty acid oxidation and glucose uptake from the blood. (McArdle 病 (V 型糖原贮积症) 是由骨骼肌糖原磷酸化酶缺乏引起的，导致肌肉糖原异化受损，引起运动不耐受、肌肉痉挛、肌红蛋白尿和“第二风”现象，后者是由于对脂肪酸氧化和血液葡萄糖摄取的依赖性增加。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 32-year-old male presents to the emergency department complaining of severe muscle pain, cramping, and dark-colored urine following a strenuous soccer game. He reports that these symptoms typically begin within 15-20 minutes of intense exercise and are relieved by rest. He denies any history of trauma or recent illness. His vital signs are stable. Physical examination reveals tender, swollen calf muscles bilaterally. Laboratory studies show a markedly elevated serum creatine kinase (CK) level (15,000 U/L; normal < 200 U/L) and positive urine dipstick for blood, but negative for heme on microscopic examination (suggesting myoglobinuria). A muscle biopsy is performed, revealing normal glycogen stores but a lack of phosphorylase activity staining. Genetic testing confirms a homozygous mutation in the *PYGM* gene.

Which of the following is the most likely underlying mechanism responsible for this patient's symptoms?

(A) Deficiency in the glucose-6-phosphatase enzyme, leading to impaired gluconeogenesis and hypoglycemia during fasting.
(B) Deficiency in the muscle isoform of glycogen phosphorylase, preventing the breakdown of glycogen into glucose-1-phosphate for energy production during exercise.
(C) Deficiency in the mitochondrial enzyme carnitine palmitoyltransferase I (CPT I), impairing the transport of long-chain fatty acids into mitochondria for beta-oxidation.
(D) Deficiency in the lysosomal enzyme acid alpha-glucosidase (GAA), leading to glycogen accumulation within lysosomes and causing progressive muscle weakness.
(E) Deficiency in the muscle isoform of phosphofructokinase-1 (PFK-1), impairing glycolysis downstream of fructose-6-phosphate.

#### Correct Answer: B

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 32-year-old male, suggesting an adult-onset genetic disorder.
- **Presenting Symptoms:** Severe muscle pain, cramping, and dark urine after strenuous exercise. Onset within 15-20 minutes of intense activity, relieved by rest. This pattern is classic for exercise-induced muscle damage.
- **Physical Exam:** Tender, swollen calf muscles, consistent with muscle injury (rhabdomyolysis).
- **Lab Findings:** Markedly elevated CK (15,000 U/L) indicates significant muscle cell breakdown. Positive urine dipstick for blood, negative for heme on microscopy, strongly suggests myoglobinuria (myoglobin reacts with the dipstick but is not detected on microscopy).
- **Muscle Biopsy:** Normal glycogen stores but lack of phosphorylase activity staining. This is the key diagnostic finding. Normal glycogen stores rule out defects in glycogen synthesis or storage, while the lack of phosphorylase activity points directly to a defect in glycogen breakdown.
- **Genetic Testing:** Homozygous mutation in the *PYGM* gene confirms the diagnosis. The *PYGM* gene encodes the muscle isoform of glycogen phosphorylase (myophosphorylase).
- **Diagnosis:** The clinical presentation, lab findings, biopsy results, and genetic confirmation are all consistent with McArdle disease (Glycogen Storage Disease Type V).
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the underlying mechanism.

##### 2. Pathophysiological, Molecular & Pharmacological Deep-Dive (病理生理、分子机制与药理靶点深度剖析)
- McArdle disease is caused by a deficiency of the muscle isoform of glycogen phosphorylase (myophosphorylase), encoded by the *PYGM* gene.
- Glycogen phosphorylase is the enzyme responsible for the rate-limiting step in glycogenolysis: the breakdown of glycogen (a polymer of glucose) into glucose-1-phosphate.
- In skeletal muscle, glycogen serves as a readily available energy source during exercise. When muscle activity increases, glycogen phosphorylase is activated to break down glycogen, providing glucose-1-phosphate, which is then converted to glucose-6-phosphate and enters glycolysis to generate ATP.
- In McArdle disease, the deficiency of myophosphorylase prevents this breakdown. During intense exercise, muscle cells cannot efficiently access the stored glycogen for energy.
- This leads to ATP depletion, muscle cramps, pain, and eventually muscle cell damage (rhabdomyolysis), releasing intracellular contents like myoglobin and CK into the bloodstream. Myoglobinuria causes the dark urine.
- The "second-wind phenomenon" occurs because, after the initial period of intense exercise where glycogen is rapidly depleted, the muscle shifts its metabolism to rely more heavily on fatty acid oxidation (beta-oxidation) and glucose uptake from the blood (which is still supplied by the liver).

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) Deficiency in glucose-6-phosphatase: This enzyme is primarily found in the liver and kidney and is involved in the final step of gluconeogenesis and glycogenolysis (converting glucose-6-phosphate to free glucose). Deficiency causes Von Gierke disease (GSD Type Ia), characterized by severe fasting hypoglycemia, hepatomegaly, lactic acidosis, and hyperlipidemia. It does not typically cause exercise-induced myoglobinuria or the specific biopsy findings described.
- (C) Deficiency in CPT I: Carnitine palmitoyltransferase I is required for the transport of long-chain fatty acids into the mitochondria for beta-oxidation. Deficiency causes a defect in fatty acid oxidation, leading to exercise intolerance, hypoglycemia during fasting, and rhabdomyolysis, particularly during prolonged exercise or fasting. While it shares exercise intolerance and rhabdomyolysis with McArdle disease, the underlying defect is in fat metabolism, not glycogenolysis, and the biopsy would show normal phosphorylase activity.
- (D) Deficiency in acid alpha-glucosidase (GAA): This is a lysosomal enzyme. Deficiency causes Pompe disease (GSD Type II), characterized by glycogen accumulation within lysosomes in various tissues, including muscle. It typically presents with progressive muscle weakness (infantile or late-onset forms) and cardiomyopathy. The biopsy would show lysosomal glycogen accumulation, not a lack of phosphorylase activity.
- (E) Deficiency in muscle PFK-1: Phosphofructokinase-1 is a key regulatory enzyme in glycolysis, catalyzing the conversion of fructose-6-phosphate to fructose-1,6-bisphosphate. Deficiency causes Tarui disease (GSD Type VII), characterized by exercise intolerance, muscle cramps, and myoglobinuria similar to McArdle disease. However, the underlying defect is in glycolysis, not glycogenolysis. The biopsy would show normal glycogen stores and phosphorylase activity, but impaired glycolytic flux.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点横向对比与鉴别矩阵):

| Feature                 | McArdle Disease (GSD V) | Von Gierke (GSD Ia) | CPT I Deficiency | Pompe Disease (GSD II) | Tarui Disease (GSD VII) |
| :---------------------- | :---------------------- | :------------------ | :-------------- | :-------------------- | :---------------------- |
| Defective Enzyme        | Muscle Glycogen Phosphorylase | Glucose-6-Phosphatase | CPT I           | Acid Alpha-Glucosidase | Muscle PFK-1            |
| Primary Metabolic Defect | Glycogenolysis          | Gluconeogenesis/Glycogenolysis | Fatty Acid Oxidation | Lysosomal Glycogenolysis | Glycolysis              |
| Tissue Affected         | Skeletal Muscle         | Liver, Kidney       | Muscle          | Multiple (Muscle, Heart) | Skeletal Muscle         |
| Key Symptoms            | Exercise intolerance, cramps, myoglobinuria, second wind | Fasting hypoglycemia, hepatomegaly | Hypoglycemia (fasting), rhabdomyolysis | Progressive muscle weakness | Exercise intolerance, cramps, myoglobinuria |
| Biopsy Finding          | Normal glycogen, ↓ Phosphorylase activity | Glycogen accumulation (liver) | Normal glycogen, normal phosphorylase | Lysosomal glycogen accumulation | Normal glycogen, normal phosphorylase |
| Gene Involved           | *PYGM*                  | *G6PC*              | *CPT1A*         | *GAA*                 | *PFKM*                  |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
McArdle disease (GSD V) is a classic example of a glycogen storage disease affecting skeletal muscle, caused by a deficiency in muscle glycogen phosphorylase. Remember the characteristic exercise-induced symptoms (pain, cramps, myoglobinuria), the "second-wind phenomenon," and the specific biopsy finding of normal glycogen stores with absent phosphorylase activity. Differentiate it from other GSDs and metabolic myopathies based on the affected enzyme, metabolic pathway, and tissue involvement.

<a id='question-021'></a>

### Question 21: Niemann-Pick Disease Type A/B
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Lysosomal storage disorders involving sphingomyelin accumulation due to deficient acid sphingomyelinase (ASM) activity, leading to characteristic clinical features and histopathology. (溶酶体贮积症，涉及由于酸性鞘氨醇酶缺乏导致鞘氨醇积累，从而引起特征性临床表现和组织病理学改变。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 6-month-old infant presents with progressive developmental delay, failure to thrive, and hepatosplenomegaly. Vital signs show mild tachycardia (140 bpm) and normal blood pressure. Physical examination reveals significant enlargement of the liver and spleen, hypotonia, and a macular "cherry-red spot" on fundoscopic examination. Laboratory tests show elevated liver enzymes (AST 150 U/L, ALT 120 U/L; normal <40 U/L) and hyperlipidemia. A bone marrow biopsy reveals numerous foamy macrophages containing lipid droplets. Genetic testing confirms a homozygous mutation in the *SMPD1* gene.

Which of the following is the most likely underlying defect leading to the accumulation of sphingomyelin in this patient?

(A) Deficiency in glucocerebrosidase leading to accumulation of glucocerebroside in macrophages.
(B) Deficiency in hexosaminidase A leading to accumulation of GM2 ganglioside in neurons.
(C) Deficiency in acid sphingomyelinase leading to accumulation of sphingomyelin in lysosomes.
(D) Deficiency in mannosidase-alpha B leading to accumulation of mannose-rich oligosaccharides in lysosomes.
(E) Deficiency in beta-galactosidase leading to accumulation of globotriaosylceramide (GM3 ganglioside) in lysosomes.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 6-month-old infant. This age is typical for the onset of severe lysosomal storage diseases like Niemann-Pick Type A.
- **Symptoms:** Progressive developmental delay, failure to thrive, hepatosplenomegaly, hypotonia. These are classic signs of severe lysosomal storage disorders affecting multiple organ systems, particularly the CNS and reticuloendothelial system.
- **Physical Exam:** Hepatosplenomegaly (organomegaly due to lipid accumulation in macrophages), hypotonia (neurological involvement), cherry-red spot (macular finding often seen in lysosomal storage diseases affecting the retina, specifically the ganglion cells).
- **Labs:** Elevated liver enzymes (hepatocellular damage/dysfunction), hyperlipidemia (often associated with lysosomal storage diseases).
- **Biopsy:** Bone marrow biopsy showing foamy macrophages containing lipid droplets. This is a hallmark finding in Niemann-Pick disease, representing lipid-laden macrophages (Niemann-Pick cells).
- **Genetics:** Homozygous mutation in the *SMPD1* gene. This gene encodes acid sphingomyelinase (ASM).
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the underlying defect causing sphingomyelin accumulation.

The combination of early onset, severe neurological symptoms (developmental delay, hypotonia), hepatosplenomegaly, cherry-red spot, foamy macrophages, and a mutation in the *SMPD1* gene strongly points to Niemann-Pick disease Type A or B. The question specifically asks for the defect causing sphingomyelin accumulation.

##### 2. Pathophysiological, Molecular & Pharmacological Deep-Dive (病理生理、分子机制与药理靶点深度剖析)
- Niemann-Pick disease is a group of inherited metabolic disorders characterized by the accumulation of sphingomyelin within lysosomes.
- Sphingomyelin is a complex lipid found in cell membranes, particularly in the myelin sheath of nerve cells.
- The breakdown of sphingomyelin occurs within lysosomes via the enzyme acid sphingomyelinase (ASM), encoded by the *SMPD1* gene.
- In Niemann-Pick disease Types A and B, there is a deficiency in ASM activity due to mutations in *SMPD1*.
- This deficiency leads to the inability to hydrolyze sphingomyelin into ceramide and phosphorylcholine.
- Consequently, sphingomyelin accumulates within lysosomes, particularly in cells of the mononuclear phagocyte system (macrophages), neurons, and hepatocytes.
- The accumulation causes cellular dysfunction and organ damage, leading to the clinical manifestations observed (hepatosplenomegaly, neurological deterioration, cherry-red spot).
- The foamy appearance of macrophages on biopsy is due to the large lipid vacuoles filled with accumulated sphingomyelin.

##### 3. Linking the Clues to the Correct Answer
- The patient's presentation (early onset, neurodegeneration, hepatosplenomegaly, cherry-red spot, foamy macrophages) is characteristic of Niemann-Pick disease.
- The genetic finding (*SMPD1* mutation) confirms the diagnosis.
- The *SMPD1* gene encodes acid sphingomyelinase (ASM).
- Therefore, the underlying defect is the deficiency of ASM, leading to sphingomyelin accumulation. Option (C) directly describes this defect.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) Deficiency in glucocerebrosidase leading to accumulation of glucocerebroside in macrophages. This describes Gaucher disease. Gaucher disease typically presents with hepatosplenomegaly, bone disease, and cytopenias. While macrophages are involved ("Gaucher cells"), they are not typically described as "foamy" in the same way as Niemann-Pick cells, and a cherry-red spot is not characteristic. The enzyme involved is glucocerebrosidase, not sphingomyelinase.
- (B) Deficiency in hexosaminidase A leading to accumulation of GM2 ganglioside in neurons. This describes Tay-Sachs disease. Tay-Sachs disease is characterized by severe neurodegeneration, a cherry-red spot, but typically lacks significant hepatosplenomegaly or foamy macrophages in the bone marrow. The accumulated substance is GM2 ganglioside, not sphingomyelin.
- (D) Deficiency in mannosidase-alpha B leading to accumulation of mannose-rich oligosaccharides in lysosomes. This describes Mannosidosis. Mannosidosis presents with various features depending on the type (alpha, beta, gamma), including coarse facial features, skeletal abnormalities, hearing loss, and developmental delay. Hepatosplenomegaly can occur, but the characteristic findings of Niemann-Pick (foamy macrophages, cherry-red spot, *SMPD1* mutation) are absent. The accumulated substance is mannose-rich oligosaccharides, not sphingomyelin.
- (E) Deficiency in beta-galactosidase leading to accumulation of globotriaosylceramide (GM3 ganglioside) in lysosomes. This describes GM1 gangliosidosis (beta-galactosidase deficiency) or Morquio B syndrome (beta-galactosidase deficiency). GM1 gangliosidosis presents with neurodegeneration, hepatosplenomegaly, skeletal abnormalities, and sometimes a cherry-red spot. However, the specific enzyme deficiency is beta-galactosidase, the accumulated substance is GM3 ganglioside (in GM1 gangliosidosis), and the genetic defect is not in *SMPD1*.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点横向对比与鉴别矩阵):

| Feature                 | Niemann-Pick (Type A/B) | Gaucher Disease        | Tay-Sachs Disease      | Mannosidosis          | GM1 Gangliosidosis    |
| :---------------------- | :---------------------- | :--------------------- | :-------------------- | :-------------------- | :-------------------- |
| **Enzyme Deficiency**   | Acid Sphingomyelinase   | Glucocerebrosidase     | Hexosaminidase A      | Mannosidase-alpha B  | Beta-Galactosidase   |
| **Gene**                | *SMPD1*                 | *GBA1*                 | *HEXA*                | *MANB*                | *GLB1*                |
| **Accumulated Substance** | Sphingomyelin           | Glucocerebroside       | GM2 Ganglioside       | Mannose Oligosaccharides | GM3 Ganglioside       |
| **Cellular Hallmark**   | Foamy Macrophages       | Gaucher Cells          | Neuronal Accumulation | Lysosomal Inclusions | Lysosomal Inclusions |
| **Hepatosplenomegaly**  | Yes                     | Yes                   | No                   | Variable              | Yes                   |
| **Neurodegeneration**   | Yes (esp. Type A)       | No (usually)           | Yes                  | Yes                   | Yes                   |
| **Cherry-Red Spot**     | Yes                     | No                    | Yes                  | No                    | Variable              |
| **Typical Age Onset**   | Infancy (Type A)        | Variable               | Infancy               | Variable              | Infancy/Childhood    |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Niemann-Pick disease (Types A and B) is caused by a deficiency in acid sphingomyelinase (ASM), encoded by the *SMPD1* gene, leading to sphingomyelin accumulation. Key features include hepatosplenomegaly, neurological symptoms (especially in Type A), foamy macrophages, and often a cherry-red spot. Differentiating it from other lysosomal storage diseases like Gaucher (glucocerebrosidase deficiency, Gaucher cells), Tay-Sachs (hexosaminidase A deficiency, GM2 accumulation), and GM1 gangliosidosis (beta-galactosidase deficiency, GM3 accumulation) relies on recognizing the specific enzyme defect, accumulated substance, and characteristic clinical/histopathological findings.

<a id='question-022'></a>

### Question 22: Tay-Sachs Disease
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Tay-Sachs disease is a lysosomal storage disorder caused by a deficiency of the alpha subunit of hexosaminidase A, leading to the accumulation of GM2 ganglioside primarily in neurons, resulting in progressive neurodegeneration. (泰-萨克斯病是一种溶酶体贮积症，由甲型己糖胺酶A亚单位缺乏引起，导致GM2神经节苷脂主要在神经元中积累，从而导致进行性神经退行变性。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 6-month-old female infant, born to first-cousin parents of Ashkenazi Jewish descent, presents with developmental delay, exaggerated startle response to loud noises, and progressive muscle weakness. Her vital signs are normal. Physical examination reveals hypotonia, nystagmus, and optic atrophy. Funduscopic examination shows a characteristic cherry-red spot in the macula bilaterally. Neurological examination demonstrates hyperreflexia and seizures. Laboratory testing shows normal liver function tests and no hepatosplenomegaly. A skin biopsy reveals fibroblasts containing numerous lysosomes with concentric whorled membranes, resembling an "onion-skin" appearance on electron microscopy. Enzyme assay of leukocytes shows markedly reduced hexosaminidase A activity (less than 5% of normal).

Which of the following is the underlying molecular defect responsible for this patient's condition?

(A) Deficiency of the beta subunit of hexosaminidase A, leading to accumulation of GM3 ganglioside.
(B) Deficiency of the alpha subunit of hexosaminidase A, leading to accumulation of GM2 ganglioside.
(C) Deficiency of glucocerebrosidase, leading to accumulation of glucocerebroside and Gaucher cells.
(D) Deficiency of sphingomyelinase, leading to accumulation of sphingomyelin and sea-blue histiocytes.
(E) Deficiency of alpha-mannosidase, leading to accumulation of mannose-rich oligosaccharides and skeletal abnormalities.

#### Correct Answer: B

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Demographics:** 6-month-old female, Ashkenazi Jewish descent, first-cousin parents. This demographic strongly suggests an autosomal recessive genetic disorder prevalent in this population, such as Tay-Sachs disease.
- **Presenting Symptoms:** Developmental delay, exaggerated startle response, progressive muscle weakness, hypotonia, nystagmus, optic atrophy, hyperreflexia, seizures. These are classic signs of progressive neurodegeneration in infancy.
- **Physical Examination:** Absence of hepatosplenomegaly is crucial. This helps differentiate Tay-Sachs from other lysosomal storage diseases like Gaucher disease or Niemann-Pick disease, which often involve visceral organomegaly.
- **Funduscopic Examination:** Cherry-red spot in the macula is a hallmark finding in Tay-Sachs disease (and some other conditions like Niemann-Pick type A and Sandhoff disease). It results from the contrast between the pale, lipid-laden retinal ganglion cells and the normal red color of the underlying choroid.
- **Skin Biopsy (Electron Microscopy):** "Onion-skin" lysosomes are characteristic of lysosomal storage disorders where undegraded substrate accumulates within lysosomes, leading to concentric layering. This finding is seen in Tay-Sachs disease.
- **Enzyme Assay:** Markedly reduced hexosaminidase A activity confirms the specific enzyme deficiency.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the underlying molecular defect.

##### 2. Pathophysiological, Molecular & Pharmacological Deep-Dive (病理生理、分子机制与药理靶点深度剖析)
- Tay-Sachs disease is an autosomal recessive lysosomal storage disorder.
- The defect lies in the enzyme hexosaminidase A (Hex A).
- Hex A is a heterodimer composed of an alpha (α) and a beta (β) subunit.
- The gene responsible for the α subunit is *HEXA*, located on chromosome 15.
- The gene responsible for the β subunit is *HEXB*, located on chromosome 1.
- In classic infantile Tay-Sachs disease, mutations in the *HEXA* gene lead to a deficiency or absence of the functional α subunit.
- The β subunit can still be produced, but it is unstable and rapidly degraded, or it forms non-functional dimers.
- The deficiency of Hex A prevents the hydrolysis of GM2 ganglioside (N-acetylgalactosamine linked to galactosylceramide) into galactosamine and galactosylceramide.
- GM2 ganglioside accumulates primarily within the lysosomes of neurons, particularly in the central nervous system, leading to neuronal dysfunction and death.
- The accumulation causes progressive neurodegeneration, manifesting as developmental regression, motor deficits, seizures, blindness, and ultimately death, typically by age 4-5 years.
- The "cherry-red spot" is due to the accumulation of GM2 ganglioside in the retinal ganglion cells, making them appear opaque white, contrasting with the normal red color of the choroid seen through the fovea.
- "Onion-skin" lysosomes are seen on electron microscopy due to the concentric layering of undegraded GM2 ganglioside within the lysosome.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): Deficiency of the beta subunit of hexosaminidase A leads to Sandhoff disease. Sandhoff disease also involves GM2 ganglioside accumulation, cherry-red spot, and onion-skin lysosomes, but it typically presents with more severe visceral involvement (hepatosplenomegaly) and often earlier onset than classic Tay-Sachs. The enzyme assay would show deficient Hex A *and* Hex B activity. This option incorrectly identifies the deficient subunit and the accumulated substrate (GM3 is incorrect; it's GM2).
- (C): Deficiency of glucocerebrosidase leads to Gaucher disease. Gaucher disease involves the accumulation of glucocerebroside, primarily in macrophages (Gaucher cells), leading to hepatosplenomegaly, bone disease, and sometimes neurological symptoms (in types 2 and 3). It does not typically cause a cherry-red spot or onion-skin lysosomes. The enzyme assay would show deficient glucocerebrosidase activity.
- (D): Deficiency of sphingomyelinase leads to Niemann-Pick disease type A or B. Niemann-Pick type A involves the accumulation of sphingomyelin, primarily in macrophages (sea-blue histiocytes), leading to hepatosplenomegaly, neurodegeneration, and often a cherry-red spot. Niemann-Pick type B primarily involves visceral accumulation without significant neurological involvement. The enzyme assay would show deficient sphingomyelinase activity. This option incorrectly identifies the enzyme and accumulated substrate.
- (E): Deficiency of alpha-mannosidase leads to alpha-mannosidosis. This involves the accumulation of mannose-rich oligosaccharides. Clinical features include coarse facial features, skeletal abnormalities (e.g., kyphoscoliosis, claw hands), hearing loss, and variable neurological involvement. It does not typically cause a cherry-red spot or onion-skin lysosomes. The enzyme assay would show deficient alpha-mannosidase activity.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点横向对比与鉴别矩阵):

| Feature                 | Tay-Sachs Disease (Hex A Deficiency) | Sandhoff Disease (Hex B Deficiency) | Gaucher Disease (Glucocerebrosidase Deficiency) | Niemann-Pick A/B (Sphingomyelinase Deficiency) | Alpha-Mannosidosis (Alpha-Mannosidase Deficiency) |
| :---------------------- | :----------------------------------- | :---------------------------------- | :--------------------------------------------- | :-------------------------------------------- | :--------------------------------------------- |
| **Deficient Enzyme**    | Hexosaminidase A (α subunit)         | Hexosaminidase A & B (β subunit)    | Glucocerebrosidase                             | Sphingomyelinase                               | Alpha-Mannosidase                              |
| **Accumulated Substrate** | GM2 Ganglioside                      | GM2 Ganglioside                     | Glucocerebroside                               | Sphingomyelin                                 | Mannose-rich oligosaccharides                 |
| **Primary Cell Type**   | Neurons                              | Neurons, Macrophages                | Macrophages (Gaucher cells)                     | Macrophages (Sea-blue histiocytes)            | Multiple cell types                            |
| **Cherry-Red Spot**     | Yes                                  | Yes                                 | No (rarely reported)                           | Yes (Type A)                                  | No                                             |
| **Onion-Skin Lysosomes**| Yes                                  | Yes                                 | No                                             | No                                            | No                                             |
| **Hepatosplenomegaly**  | No                                   | Yes (often significant)             | Yes (prominent)                                 | Yes (prominent)                               | No                                             |
| **Inheritance**         | Autosomal Recessive                  | Autosomal Recessive                 | Autosomal Recessive                            | Autosomal Recessive                           | Autosomal Recessive                           |
| **Key Feature**         | Neurodegeneration, no visceral signs | Neurodegeneration, visceral signs   | Hepatosplenomegaly, bone disease               | Hepatosplenomegaly, neurodegeneration (Type A) | Skeletal/Facial dysmorphism, hearing loss      |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Tay-Sachs disease is caused by a deficiency in the alpha subunit of hexosaminidase A, leading to GM2 ganglioside accumulation in neurons. Key features include infantile onset, progressive neurodegeneration, exaggerated startle response, cherry-red spot, onion-skin lysosomes, and absence of hepatosplenomegaly. Differentiate it from Sandhoff disease (beta subunit deficiency, visceral involvement), Gaucher disease (glucocerebroside accumulation, hepatosplenomegaly), Niemann-Pick disease (sphingomyelin accumulation, hepatosplenomegaly, sea-blue histiocytes), and alpha-mannosidosis (mannose-rich oligosaccharides, skeletal abnormalities).

<a id='question-023'></a>

### Question 23: Fabry Disease
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Fabry disease is an X-linked lysosomal storage disorder caused by a deficiency of alpha-galactosidase A, leading to the accumulation of globotriaosylceramide (Gb3) in lysosomes, particularly in endothelial cells, podocytes, and neurons. This accumulation results in characteristic clinical features including angiokeratomas, acroparesthesia, hypohidrosis, corneal opacities, and progressive renal, cardiac, and cerebrovascular disease. (法布里病是一种X连锁溶酶体贮积症，由α-半乳糖苷酶A缺乏引起，导致Gb3（三聚糖基脑苷脂）在溶酶体中积累，尤其是在内皮细胞、足细胞和神经元中。这种积累导致特征性临床表现，包括血管瘤、肢端感觉异常、出汗减少、角膜混浊以及进行性肾脏、心脏和脑血管疾病。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 35-year-old man presents to his primary care physician complaining of burning pain and tingling in his hands and feet, which worsens with exercise or heat exposure. He also reports decreased sweating and occasional episodes of abdominal pain. Physical examination reveals multiple small, dark red-purple papules on his lower abdomen and buttocks. His renal function tests show elevated serum creatinine (1.8 mg/dL; normal range 0.6-1.2 mg/dL) and proteinuria (3+ on dipstick). A skin biopsy from one of the papules shows dilated, tortuous capillaries in the upper dermis filled with lipid-laden macrophages. Electron microscopy of a renal biopsy reveals lamellated, electron-dense deposits within the glomerular basement membrane and podocytes. Genetic testing confirms a mutation in the *GLA* gene.

Which of the following is the most likely underlying pathological mechanism responsible for the patient's renal manifestations?

(A) Deposition of amyloid fibrils derived from misfolded transthyretin protein in the glomeruli.
(B) Accumulation of globotriaosylceramide (Gb3) within the lysosomes of renal tubular epithelial cells and podocytes.
(C) Formation of immune complex deposits containing antibodies against glomerular basement membrane antigens.
(D) Progressive fibrosis and sclerosis of the glomeruli secondary to chronic hypertension and proteinuria.
(E) Impaired transport of glucose across the glomerular basement membrane due to mutations in the *SLC2A2* gene.

#### Correct Answer: B

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
*   **Patient Profile:** 35-year-old man. This age is typical for the onset of symptoms in Fabry disease, particularly in males due to its X-linked inheritance.
*   **Symptoms:** Burning pain/tingling in hands/feet (acroparesthesia), decreased sweating (hypohidrosis), abdominal pain. These are classic symptoms of Fabry disease. Acroparesthesia is often exacerbated by heat or exercise.
*   **Physical Exam:** Dark red-purple papules on abdomen/buttocks (angiokeratomas). These are pathognomonic skin lesions of Fabry disease, resulting from dilated capillaries filled with Gb3-laden macrophages.
*   **Lab Findings:** Elevated creatinine (renal insufficiency) and proteinuria. Renal involvement (Fabry nephropathy) is a major cause of morbidity and mortality in Fabry disease, often presenting as proteinuria and progressive decline in glomerular filtration rate.
*   **Skin Biopsy:** Dilated, tortuous capillaries filled with lipid-laden macrophages. This confirms the presence of angiokeratomas and suggests lipid accumulation.
*   **Renal Biopsy (Electron Microscopy):** Lamellated, electron-dense deposits within the glomerular basement membrane and podocytes. These are characteristic "zebra bodies" representing the accumulation of Gb3 within the lysosomes of these cells.
*   **Genetic Testing:** Mutation in the *GLA* gene. This gene encodes alpha-galactosidase A, confirming the diagnosis of Fabry disease.
*   **Question Stem:** Asks for the underlying pathological mechanism responsible for the *renal manifestations*.

##### 2. Pathophysiological, Molecular & Pharmacological Deep-Dive (病理生理、分子机制与药理靶点深度剖析)
*   **Fabry Disease Pathophysiology:** Fabry disease is caused by a deficiency of the lysosomal enzyme alpha-galactosidase A (encoded by the *GLA* gene on the X chromosome). This enzyme normally breaks down globotriaosylceramide (Gb3, also known as ceramide trihexoside).
*   **Gb3 Accumulation:** Lack of alpha-galactosidase A leads to the progressive accumulation of Gb3 within the lysosomes of various cells, particularly endothelial cells, smooth muscle cells, podocytes, and neurons.
*   **Renal Manifestations:** In the kidney, Gb3 accumulates primarily in podocytes and renal tubular epithelial cells. This accumulation disrupts cellular function, leading to podocyte injury, glomerular basement membrane thickening, and eventually glomerulosclerosis and interstitial fibrosis. The characteristic electron microscopy finding is the presence of lamellated, electron-dense deposits (zebra bodies) representing stored Gb3 within lysosomes. The clinical manifestations include proteinuria, progressive renal failure, and end-stage renal disease.
*   **Mechanism Link:** The question asks for the mechanism of renal manifestations. The core defect is the lack of alpha-galactosidase A, leading to Gb3 accumulation within renal cells (podocytes, tubular cells), which directly causes the observed pathology (proteinuria, renal failure, zebra bodies).

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
*   **(A) Deposition of amyloid fibrils derived from misfolded transthyretin protein in the glomeruli:** This describes amyloidosis, specifically transthyretin amyloidosis (ATTR amyloidosis), which can cause nephrotic syndrome and renal failure. However, the clinical presentation (angiokeratomas, acroparesthesia, hypohidrosis), skin biopsy findings, and specific electron microscopy findings (lamellated deposits, not amyloid fibrils) are inconsistent with ATTR amyloidosis. ATTR amyloidosis typically presents with cardiomyopathy, peripheral neuropathy, and autonomic dysfunction, but not the characteristic skin lesions of Fabry disease.
*   **(C) Formation of immune complex deposits containing antibodies against glomerular basement membrane antigens:** This describes anti-glomerular basement membrane (anti-GBM) disease (Goodpasture syndrome), an autoimmune condition causing rapidly progressive glomerulonephritis and often pulmonary hemorrhage. The clinical presentation, skin findings, and electron microscopy findings (lamellated deposits, not immune complexes) are inconsistent with anti-GBM disease. Anti-GBM disease typically presents with hematuria, proteinuria, and rapidly declining renal function, often associated with pulmonary symptoms.
*   **(D) Progressive fibrosis and sclerosis of the glomeruli secondary to chronic hypertension and proteinuria:** While fibrosis and sclerosis are *consequences* of Fabry nephropathy, they are not the *primary underlying pathological mechanism* initiated by the genetic defect. The primary mechanism is the accumulation of Gb3. Hypertension and proteinuria can contribute to fibrosis, but the initial insult is the lysosomal storage. This option describes a downstream effect rather than the root cause.
*   **(E) Impaired transport of glucose across the glomerular basement membrane due to mutations in the *SLC2A2* gene:** Mutations in *SLC2A2* (encoding GLUT2) cause renal glucosuria, characterized by glucose excretion in the urine despite normal blood glucose levels. This condition is typically benign and does not cause the systemic symptoms (acroparesthesia, angiokeratomas, hypohidrosis), renal failure, or specific electron microscopy findings seen in this patient.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点横向对比与鉴别矩阵):

| Feature                     | Fabry Disease (Correct Answer)                                  | ATTR Amyloidosis (A)                                  | Anti-GBM Disease (C)                               | Fabry Nephropathy Fibrosis (D)                       | Renal Glucosuria (E)                                  |
| :-------------------------- | :-------------------------------------------------------------- | :---------------------------------------------------- | :--------------------------------------------------- | :---------------------------------------------------- | :------------------------------------------------------ |
| **Primary Defect**          | Alpha-galactosidase A deficiency, Gb3 accumulation              | Misfolded Transthyretin Amyloid Deposition             | Anti-GBM antibodies, Immune Complex Deposition       | Secondary to chronic damage (not primary mechanism) | GLUT2 transporter defect                               |
| **Gene Involved**           | *GLA*                                                            | *TTR*                                                 | N/A (Autoimmune)                                     | N/A (Consequence)                                     | *SLC2A2*                                                |
| **Key Clinical Features**   | Angiokeratomas, Acroparesthesia, Hypohidrosis, Renal Failure    | Cardiomyopathy, Neuropathy, Amyloid Deposition        | Rapidly progressive GN, Hematuria, Pulmonary Hemorrhage | Proteinuria, Renal Failure (late stage)               | Glucosuria with normal blood glucose                  |
| **Skin Biopsy**             | Angiokeratomas (lipid-laden macrophages)                        | Amyloid deposits                                      | N/A                                                  | N/A                                                  | N/A                                                     |
| **Renal Biopsy (EM)**       | Lamellated, electron-dense deposits ("zebra bodies")            | Amyloid fibrils (randomly arranged, non-branching)    | Linear IgG deposits along GBM                         | Glomerulosclerosis, Tubulointerstitial Fibrosis       | N/A                                                     |
| **Underlying Mechanism**    | Lysosomal storage of Gb3 in renal cells (podocytes, tubules)     | Amyloid fibril deposition disrupting tissue structure | Immune-mediated attack on GBM                        | End-stage consequence of injury                       | Impaired glucose reabsorption                          |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Fabry disease is a classic X-linked lysosomal storage disorder caused by alpha-galactosidase A deficiency, leading to Gb3 accumulation. Renal involvement is common, characterized by proteinuria and progressive renal failure due to Gb3 accumulation in podocytes and tubular cells, visible as "zebra bodies" on electron microscopy. Key differentials for renal disease include amyloidosis, immune complex glomerulonephritis, and secondary fibrosis, but the specific clinical triad (angiokeratomas, acroparesthesia, hypohidrosis) and characteristic EM findings point strongly to Fabry disease.

<a id='question-024'></a>

### Question 24: Krabbe Disease
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Krabbe disease (globoid cell leukodystrophy) is an autosomal recessive lysosomal storage disorder caused by deficiency of galactocerebrosidase (GALC), leading to accumulation of psychosine (galactosylsphingosine), which is toxic to oligodendrocytes and causes demyelination. (Krabbe 病是一种常染色体隐性溶酶体贮积病，由半乳糖脑苷脂酶 (GALC) 缺乏引起，导致毒素精神苷 (galactosylsphingosine) 积累，损害少突胶质细胞并引起脱髓鞘。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 6-month-old male infant, born full-term after an uncomplicated pregnancy to non-consanguineous parents, presents with progressive irritability, feeding difficulties, and generalized hypotonia. His developmental milestones were initially normal, but he has shown regression in motor skills over the past 2 months. Physical examination reveals nystagmus, hyperreflexia, and exaggerated startle response to loud noises. Serum lactate and pyruvate levels are normal. Brain MRI shows diffuse white matter abnormalities, particularly affecting the corticospinal tracts, basal ganglia, and thalami, with T2 hyperintensity and T1 hypointensity. A skin biopsy is performed for enzyme assay and histopathology. Enzyme assay reveals markedly reduced galactocerebrosidase activity in fibroblasts. Histopathology of the skin biopsy shows multinucleated giant cells with clear cytoplasm containing lipid droplets, consistent with globoid cells, scattered within the dermis. Immunohistochemical staining for galactocerebrosidase is negative within these cells.

Which of the following is the most likely underlying pathological mechanism responsible for the observed neurological deterioration in this patient?

(A) Accumulation of sphingomyelin due to deficient sphingomyelinase activity, leading to demyelination and neuronal ceroid lipofuscinosis.
(B) Deficiency of arylsulfatase A, resulting in accumulation of sulfatides and demyelination, primarily affecting peripheral nerves.
(C) Accumulation of psychosine, a toxic metabolite, due to deficient galactocerebrosidase activity, leading to oligodendrocyte damage and demyelination.
(D) Deficiency of hexosaminidase A, causing accumulation of GM2 ganglioside within lysosomes, primarily affecting neurons in the central nervous system.
(E) Deficiency of glucocerebrosidase, leading to accumulation of glucocerebroside within macrophages, causing hepatosplenomegaly and bone marrow infiltration.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
*   **Patient Profile:** 6-month-old male infant. This age is typical for the onset of infantile Krabbe disease.
*   **Presenting Symptoms:** Progressive irritability, feeding difficulties, generalized hypotonia, developmental regression (motor skills), nystagmus, hyperreflexia, exaggerated startle response. These are classic signs of Krabbe disease, indicating severe neurological involvement.
*   **Diagnostic Studies:**
    *   **Brain MRI:** Diffuse white matter abnormalities (T2 hyperintensity, T1 hypointensity) affecting corticospinal tracts, basal ganglia, and thalami. This pattern is characteristic of leukodystrophies, including Krabbe disease.
    *   **Skin Biopsy:**
        *   **Enzyme Assay:** Markedly reduced galactocerebrosidase activity. This is the definitive biochemical finding for Krabbe disease.
        *   **Histopathology:** Multinucleated giant cells with clear cytoplasm containing lipid droplets (globoid cells) in the dermis. These are pathognomonic for Krabbe disease.
        *   **Immunohistochemistry:** Negative staining for galactocerebrosidase within globoid cells, confirming the enzyme deficiency within these cells.
*   **Question Stem:** Asks for the underlying pathological mechanism responsible for neurological deterioration.

The combination of infantile onset, progressive neurological deterioration, characteristic MRI findings, markedly reduced galactocerebrosidase activity, and the presence of globoid cells strongly points to Krabbe disease. The question asks for the mechanism. Krabbe disease is caused by a deficiency in galactocerebrosidase (GALC), which breaks down galactocerebroside and psychosine. The deficiency leads to the accumulation of psychosine, which is toxic to oligodendrocytes, the myelin-producing cells in the CNS. This toxicity results in demyelination and the characteristic neurological symptoms.

##### 2. Pathophysiological, Molecular & Pharmacological Deep-Dive (病理生理、分子机制与药理靶点深度剖析)
*   **Krabbe Disease Pathophysiology:** Krabbe disease is an autosomal recessive disorder caused by mutations in the *GALC* gene, which encodes the enzyme galactocerebrosidase (GALC). GALC is a lysosomal enzyme responsible for hydrolyzing galactocerebroside and psychosine (galactosylsphingosine).
*   **Mechanism:** Deficiency of GALC leads to the accumulation of its substrates, primarily psychosine, within lysosomes of oligodendrocytes. Psychosine is highly toxic to these cells, disrupting their function and leading to their death.
*   **Consequences:** The death of oligodendrocytes results in severe demyelination of the central nervous system white matter. This demyelination disrupts nerve impulse transmission, leading to the progressive neurological symptoms observed in Krabbe disease, including motor deficits, irritability, hypertonia/hypotonia, seizures, blindness, and developmental regression.
*   **Histopathology:** The characteristic feature is the presence of multinucleated giant cells (globoid cells) in the white matter and sometimes in the skin biopsy. These cells contain lipid-laden lysosomes filled with accumulated psychosine.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): Accumulation of sphingomyelin due to deficient sphingomyelinase activity is characteristic of Niemann-Pick disease types A and B. While it causes demyelination and neurological symptoms (Type A), the specific enzyme deficiency, accumulated substrate, and histopathology (foam cells, not globoid cells) are different. Neuronal ceroid lipofuscinosis (NCL) involves accumulation of lipopigments, not sphingomyelin.
- (B): Deficiency of arylsulfatase A leads to metachromatic leukodystrophy (MLD). This causes accumulation of sulfatides, leading to demyelination, primarily affecting peripheral nerves initially, although CNS involvement occurs later. The enzyme deficiency, accumulated substrate, and clinical presentation differ from Krabbe disease.
- (D): Deficiency of hexosaminidase A causes Tay-Sachs disease. This leads to the accumulation of GM2 ganglioside within neurons, primarily affecting the CNS. The clinical presentation (cherry-red spot on macula, progressive neurodegeneration) and the cell type affected (neurons vs. oligodendrocytes) are distinct from Krabbe disease.
- (E): Deficiency of glucocerebrosidase causes Gaucher disease. This leads to the accumulation of glucocerebroside within macrophages (Gaucher cells), primarily affecting the reticuloendothelial system (liver, spleen, bone marrow). While neurological involvement can occur (Type 3), the primary pathology involves macrophage infiltration, not oligodendrocyte damage and demyelination, and the accumulated substrate is different.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点横向对比与鉴别矩阵):

| Feature                 | Krabbe Disease (Globoid Cell Leukodystrophy) | Niemann-Pick Disease (Type A/B) | Metachromatic Leukodystrophy (MLD) | Tay-Sachs Disease | Gaucher Disease |
| :---------------------- | :------------------------------------------- | :----------------------------- | :---------------------------------- | :---------------- | :-------------- |
| **Enzyme Deficiency**   | Galactocerebrosidase (GALC)                  | Sphingomyelinase               | Arylsulfatase A                     | Hexosaminidase A  | Glucocerebrosidase |
| **Accumulated Substrate** | Psychosine (Galactosylsphingosine)           | Sphingomyelin                  | Sulfatides                          | GM2 Ganglioside   | Glucocerebroside |
| **Primary Cell Affected** | Oligodendrocytes                             | Macrophages, Neurons           | Schwann Cells, Oligodendrocytes    | Neurons           | Macrophages      |
| **Key Histopathology**  | Globoid Cells (Multinucleated giant cells)   | Foam Cells                    | Metachromatic Granules              | None specific     | Gaucher Cells    |
| **Typical Onset**       | Infancy (3-6 months)                          | Infancy/Childhood              | Infancy/Childhood                   | Infancy           | Variable         |
| **Key Clinical Features** | Irritability, Hypotonia, Hyperreflexia, Demyelination | Hepatosplenomegaly, Neurodegeneration | Peripheral Neuropathy, Demyelination | Cherry-Red Spot, Neurodegeneration | Hepatosplenomegaly, Bone Pain |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Krabbe disease is caused by GALC deficiency leading to psychosine accumulation and oligodendrocyte toxicity, resulting in demyelination. Key features include infantile onset, progressive neurological decline, characteristic MRI findings, and pathognomonic globoid cells. Differentiate it from other lysosomal storage diseases like Niemann-Pick (sphingomyelinase deficiency, foam cells), MLD (arylsulfatase A deficiency, sulfatides), Tay-Sachs (hexosaminidase A deficiency, GM2 ganglioside, neurons), and Gaucher (glucocerebrosidase deficiency, glucocerebroside, macrophages).
