<a id='question-050'></a>

### Question 050: Refsum Disease
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Refsum disease is an autosomal recessive peroxisomal biogenesis disorder caused by mutations in the *PHYH* gene, leading to a deficiency in phytanoyl-CoA hydroxylase. This enzyme is crucial for the alpha-oxidation of phytanic acid, a branched-chain fatty acid derived from dietary sources (e.g., dairy, meat). The resulting accumulation of phytanic acid in tissues, particularly the nervous system and retina, causes neurological symptoms (ataxia, peripheral neuropathy) and ophthalmological findings (retinitis pigmentosa). (Refsum 病是一种常染色体隐性过氧化物酶体生物合成障碍，由 *PHYH* 基因突变引起，导致植物烷酰辅酶 A 水氧化酶缺乏。该酶对于植物烷酸的α-氧化至关重要，植物烷酸是一种从饮食中摄取的支链脂肪酸。植物烷酸在组织（特别是神经系统和视网膜）中的积累会导致神经系统症状（共济失调、周围神经病）和眼科发现（色素性视网膜炎）。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 7-year-old boy presents to the pediatric neurology clinic with progressive gait ataxia and difficulty with fine motor skills over the past year. His parents report that he frequently falls and has trouble keeping up with his peers during physical activities. He also complains of blurred vision, especially at night. On physical examination, he exhibits truncal ataxia, dysmetria, and decreased deep tendon reflexes in the lower extremities. Fundoscopic examination reveals characteristic "salt and pepper" pigmentation changes in the retina. Laboratory investigations show elevated levels of phytanic acid (45 µmol/L; normal < 5 µmol/L) in plasma and markedly elevated levels of phytanic acid in erythrocytes (1500 nmol/L/RBC; normal < 150 nmol/L/RBC). Liver function tests and creatine kinase levels are normal. Genetic testing confirms compound heterozygous mutations in the *PHYH* gene.

Which of the following is the underlying biochemical defect responsible for the patient's clinical presentation?

(A) Deficiency of very long-chain acyl-CoA dehydrogenase (VLCAD), leading to impaired beta-oxidation of very long-chain fatty acids.
(B) Deficiency of branched-chain alpha-keto acid dehydrogenase complex, resulting in accumulation of branched-chain amino acids.
(C) Deficiency of phytanoyl-CoA hydroxylase, impairing alpha-oxidation of phytanic acid.
(D) Deficiency of sphingomyelinase, causing accumulation of sphingomyelin in lysosomes.
(E) Deficiency of galactocerebrosidase, leading to accumulation of galactocerebroside in oligodendrocytes.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 7-year-old boy. Age of onset is typical for Refsum disease.
- **Presenting Symptoms:** Progressive gait ataxia, difficulty with fine motor skills, blurred vision (especially at night). These are classic neurological and ophthalmological symptoms of Refsum disease. Ataxia points to cerebellar or sensory pathway involvement. Night blindness suggests retinitis pigmentosa.
- **Physical Examination:** Truncal ataxia, dysmetria, decreased deep tendon reflexes. These findings further support a neurological disorder affecting coordination and peripheral nerves.
- **Fundoscopic Examination:** "Salt and pepper" pigmentation changes in the retina. This is a pathognomonic finding for Refsum disease (retinitis pigmentosa).
- **Laboratory Findings:** Elevated plasma phytanic acid (45 µmol/L) and markedly elevated erythrocyte phytanic acid (1500 nmol/L/RBC). These are diagnostic biochemical markers for Refsum disease. Normal LFTs and CK rule out other metabolic disorders affecting the liver or muscle significantly.
- **Genetic Testing:** Compound heterozygous mutations in the *PHYH* gene. This confirms the diagnosis of Refsum disease, as *PHYH* encodes phytanoyl-CoA hydroxylase.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the underlying biochemical defect.

##### 2. Pathophysiological, Molecular & Pharmacological Deep-Dive (病理生理、分子机制与药理靶点深度剖析)
- The patient's symptoms (ataxia, retinitis pigmentosa) and biochemical findings (elevated phytanic acid) strongly suggest Refsum disease.
- Refsum disease is caused by a defect in the alpha-oxidation pathway within peroxisomes.
- The specific enzyme deficient in classic Refsum disease is phytanoyl-CoA hydroxylase (encoded by the *PHYH* gene).
- This enzyme catalyzes the first step in the alpha-oxidation of phytanic acid, converting phytanoyl-CoA to 2-hydroxyphytanoyl-CoA.
- Phytanic acid is a branched-chain fatty acid obtained from the diet (e.g., dairy products, meat, eggs). Humans cannot metabolize it via beta-oxidation due to the methyl group branching at the beta-carbon.
- Alpha-oxidation is the pathway required to shorten phytanic acid, allowing its subsequent beta-oxidation.
- Deficiency of phytanoyl-CoA hydroxylase leads to the accumulation of phytanic acid in plasma, erythrocytes, and tissues, particularly the brain, retina, and peripheral nerves.
- The accumulation of phytanic acid is toxic to these tissues, causing the characteristic neurological and ophthalmological manifestations.
- The vignette provides all the necessary clues (clinical presentation, fundoscopy, elevated phytanic acid, *PHYH* gene mutation) to pinpoint the specific enzyme deficiency.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) Deficiency of very long-chain acyl-CoA dehydrogenase (VLCAD): VLCAD deficiency is a disorder of peroxisomal beta-oxidation, affecting the breakdown of very long-chain fatty acids (VLCFAs). It typically presents with hypotonia, cardiomyopathy, liver dysfunction, and hypoglycemia, often in infancy. While it's a peroxisomal disorder, it doesn't involve phytanic acid metabolism and presents differently. Elevated VLCFAs, not phytanic acid, would be the hallmark.
- (B) Deficiency of branched-chain alpha-keto acid dehydrogenase complex: This enzyme complex is involved in the metabolism of branched-chain amino acids (leucine, isoleucine, valine). Deficiency leads to maple syrup urine disease (MSUD), characterized by accumulation of branched-chain amino acids and their corresponding alpha-keto acids. Symptoms include poor feeding, vomiting, lethargy, neurological deterioration, and a characteristic maple syrup odor in urine and cerumen. This is unrelated to phytanic acid or Refsum disease.
- (D) Deficiency of sphingomyelinase: This enzyme deficiency causes Niemann-Pick disease type A and B. It leads to the accumulation of sphingomyelin in lysosomes, primarily affecting the liver, spleen, brain, and bone marrow. Symptoms include hepatosplenomegaly, neurodegeneration, and failure to thrive. It is a lysosomal storage disorder, not a peroxisomal disorder, and involves sphingolipid metabolism, not phytanic acid.
- (E) Deficiency of galactocerebrosidase: This enzyme deficiency causes Krabbe disease (globoid cell leukodystrophy), a lysosomal storage disorder affecting myelin-producing cells (oligodendrocytes and Schwann cells). It leads to the accumulation of galactocerebroside and psychosine. Symptoms typically begin in infancy and include irritability, hypertonia, developmental delay, seizures, and progressive neurodegeneration. It is unrelated to phytanic acid metabolism or Refsum disease.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点横向对比与鉴别矩阵):

| Feature                 | Refsum Disease (PHYH deficiency) | VLCAD Deficiency          | MSUD (Branched-chain α-keto acid dehydrogenase deficiency) | Niemann-Pick A/B (Sphingomyelinase deficiency) | Krabbe Disease (Galactocerebrosidase deficiency) |
| :---------------------- | :-------------------------------- | :------------------------ | :------------------------------------------------------ | :--------------------------------------------- | :---------------------------------------------- |
| **Primary Defect**      | Phytanoyl-CoA Hydroxylase         | VLCAD                    | Branched-chain α-keto acid dehydrogenase complex        | Sphingomyelinase                               | Galactocerebrosidase                           |
| **Metabolic Pathway**   | Peroxisomal α-oxidation           | Peroxisomal β-oxidation  | Amino acid metabolism                                   | Lysosomal lipid metabolism                      | Lysosomal lipid metabolism                      |
| **Accumulating Substance** | Phytanic acid                     | VLCFAs                   | Branched-chain amino acids & α-keto acids               | Sphingomyelin                                  | Galactocerebroside & Psychosine                 |
| **Key Clinical Features** | Ataxia, Retinitis Pigmentosa      | Hypotonia, Cardiomyopathy | Maple syrup odor, Neurological deterioration          | Hepatosplenomegaly, Neurodegeneration          | Infantile neurodegeneration, Hypertonia         |
| **Diagnostic Marker**   | Elevated Phytanic Acid            | Elevated VLCFAs           | Elevated Branched-chain Amino Acids & α-keto acids      | Low Sphingomyelinase activity, ↑ Sphingomyelin | Low Galactocerebrosidase activity, ↑ Galactocerebroside |
| **Inheritance**         | Autosomal Recessive               | Autosomal Recessive       | Autosomal Recessive                                     | Autosomal Recessive                            | Autosomal Recessive                            |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Refsum disease is a classic example of a peroxisomal disorder affecting alpha-oxidation, leading to phytanic acid accumulation. Remember the key clinical triad of ataxia, retinitis pigmentosa, and elevated phytanic acid levels. Differentiate it from other peroxisomal disorders like VLCAD deficiency (affects beta-oxidation, presents with hypotonia/cardiomyopathy) and lysosomal storage disorders like Niemann-Pick (sphingomyelin accumulation, hepatosplenomegaly) and Krabbe (galactocerebroside accumulation, infantile neurodegeneration). Also, distinguish it from amino acid metabolism disorders like MSUD (branched-chain amino acid accumulation, maple syrup odor).
